The only move that could attack g5 is g2g3, but g2 is pinned.
The only way to unpin g2 is to move the king from h1.
The available king moves are h1g1. (Wait, h1g2 is not possible because g2 is occupied).
So h1g1 is the only way to unpin g2 and subsequently attack the queen.

Wait, let me check the legal moves one more time:
h1g1, f1g1, f1e1, f1d1, f1c1, f1b1, f1a1, h2h3, f2f3, c2c3, a2a3, h2h4, f2f4, a2a4.
Yes, h1g1 is there.

Is f1g1 better?
If f1g1, the Rook now defends g2.
But the king is still at h1, so the pin on g2 still exists (even though g2 is now double-defended).
And f2 becomes undefended.
If ... Bxf2, then gxf2? No, the pawn at g2 is pinned!
Wait... if f1g1, and Black plays ... Bxf2.
The pawn at g2 cannot take f2 because it's pinned by the queen at g5.
So ... Bxf2 is just a free pawn and an attack on the rook at g1? No, the rook is at g1.
So f1g1 is definitely worse than h1g1.

What about f2f3?
If f2f3, White defends the f2 square.
But the pin on g2 remains.
And White's king is still at h1.
If Black continues with ... h5, ... f5, etc., White is still paralyzed.

So h1g1 seems like the best move. It unpins the g-pawn and prepares g2g3.
Let's consider if there's any immediate response to h1g1 that is devastating.
h1g1 ... Bxf2. Rxf2.
h1g1 ... Qxg2+ Kxg2.
h1g1 ... Qd5.
h1g1 ... d5.
None of these seem to be immediate game-enders.

One last look at the board.
Row 4 (index 3): . . ♝ . . . ♛ .  (c5 Bishop, g5 Queen)
Row 3 (index 2): . ♟ ♝ . . . ♟ .  (b6 Pawn, c6 Bishop, g6 Pawn)
Wait, is the bishop at c6 attacking anything?
c6-g2 is a diagonal.
c6 -> d5 -> e4 -> f3 -> g2.
Wait! The bishop at c6 ALSO attacks g2!
So g2 is attacked by the Queen at g5 AND the Bishop at c6.
And it's defended by the King at h1.
If White plays h1g1, the king is no longer at h1.
Now g2 is defended by... the Rook at f1? No.
So if h1g1, g2 is now undefended!
Wait, I missed this!
Let me re-calculate.
King h1, Rook f1, Pawn g2.
Black Queen g5, Black Bishop c6.
If White plays h1g1:
The King is now at g1.
The Queen at g5 and the Bishop at c6 are both attacking g2.
The King at g1 is now defending g2.
So g2 is still defended!
And since the king is no longer at h1, the pawn at g2 is no longer pinned!
So if h1g1, g2 is defended by the king at g1 and it is no longer pinned.
This means g2g3 is now possible!

Let's re-verify the attackers of g2.
Queen at g5: attacking g2.
Bishop at c6: attacking g2.
Total attackers: 2.
Defenders of g2:
King at h1: defending g2.
If White plays h1g1:
Defenders of g2:
King at g1: defending g2.
So g2 is still defended by one piece.
Wait, if there are two attackers (Q and B) and only one defender (K), then g2 is under-defended.
Is it?
In chess, a piece is defended if at least one piece protects it.
But if the opponent can capture it and the recapture is not possible, then it's a loss.
If Black plays ... Bxg2, White plays Kxg2.
If Black plays ... Qxg2, White plays Kxg2.
So g2 is safe as long as the king can recapture.

So h1g1 still seems like a solid move.
It breaks the pin and mantém the defense of g2.

Let's double check f2 again.
Bishop c5 attacks f2.
Rook f1 defends f2.
If h1g1, Rook f1 still defends f2.
If ... Bxf2, then Rxf2.
Everything seems to hold up.

Final decision: h1g1.
